                                     Appendix C

         Field-Expedient Survey Techniques

    Surveyed locations may be provided by the artillery survey personnel.
    Normally, a map spot location to six-digit or eight-digit grid coordinates is
    estimated by the platoon supervisor that is the most qualified. With the
    “roving mortars” concept, new methods of position location are needed.
    Two such methods are described in this appendix. The mortar position
    should be constantly improved to include a more accurate platoon center
    location.

GRAPHIC RESECTION

     C-1. A graphic resection can be used to establish the coordinates of a point or to check
     the accuracy of a map spot. If the resection cannot be performed from platoon center,
     the platoon center coordinates can be estimated based on the coordinates of the nearby
     resected point. The platoon may be required to locate its roving gun (split section) and
     primary, alternate, or supplementary positions as accurately as possible. Often, the
     location of those positions can be determined by a simple map spot location. Whenever
     possible, a more accurate method of location should be used. Graphic resection is a
     simple method using the aiming circle, tracing paper, and a map. Follow these steps:

          • Identify three distant points that appear on a map (see figure C-1).
          • With an aiming circle, measure the azimuth to those points, and preferably, the

                angles between the points should be greater than 400 mils.
          • On tracing paper, place a dot representing the aiming circle location.

                     Figure C-1. Three distant points
• Draw a line from this dot in any direction (see figure C-2, page 478).

29 August 2025  TC 3-22.91 (INCL C1)                                      477


---

Appendix C

                           Figure C-2. Line drawn in any direction
          • With a protractor aligned with the correct azimuth on the line (see figure C-3),

                draw two lines from the dot on the measured azimuths (see figure C-4).

     Figure C-3. Protractor aligned with correct azimuth

478  TC 3-22.91 (INCL C1)  29 August 2025


---

                                                            Field-Expedient Survey Techniques

             Figure C-4. Two more lines drawn from dot
• Place the tracing paper over the map of the area and slide it around until it is

     positioned so that the three lines pass through their respective distant points
     (see figure C-5).
• The dot on the tracing paper represents the location of the aiming circle (mortar
     position) on the map.

                Figure C-5. Positioning of tracing paper

29 August 2025  TC 3-22.91 (INCL C1)                      479


---

Appendix C

          • If the angles are plotted with a standard protractor (accurate to about 10 mils)
                and oriented over a 1:50,000 scale map, the resection should be accurate within
                100 meters.

HASTY SURVEY

     C-2. A terrain feature or man-made object is needed close to the desired mortar position
     for a hasty survey. This identifies the mortar position on a map by eight-digit grid
     coordinates. The hasty survey begins at this point, using the pivot point of the M16
     plotting board to represent the selected known position. (See figure C-6.)

                                      Figure C-6. Hasty survey

STEP 1

     C-3. To begin the hasty survey, set the M2A2 aiming circle over the known point, level
     it, index the declination constant using the azimuth micrometer knob, and, with the
     nonrecording (lower) motion, orient the magnetic needle toward north. Now the grid
     azimuth can be measured.

STEP 2

     C-4. While the “circle” Soldier is measuring the grid’s azimuth, an assistant (the “post”
     Soldier) moves toward the desired mortar position with the two aiming posts. Before
     moving, the “post” Soldier will have joined the posts together and placed reflective or
     black tape strips exactly 2 meters apart on each post. Thus, the post becomes a subtense
     bar. (See figure C-7.)

480  TC 3-22.91 (INCL C1)  29 August 2025


---

                Field-Expedient Survey Techniques

                                     Figure C-7. Subtense bar

STEP 3

     C-5. At this point, the first leg of the hasty survey can be done. The “circle” Soldier
     directs the “post” Soldier to move toward the desired mortar position until the “post”
     Soldier is within 290 meters and to place the post into the ground. This point on the
     ground becomes traverse station-1 (TS-1).

STEP 4

     C-6. The “circle” Soldier then rotates the azimuth motion (upper motion) until the
     vertical crossline in the telescope is on the center of the post. The “circle” Soldier records
     the azimuth to the post and labels it traverse leg 1. (See figure C-8, page 482.)

29 August 2025  TC 3-22.91 (INCL C1)  481


---

Appendix C

                                      Figure C-8. Traverse leg

STEP 5

     C-7. Next, the “post” Soldier removes the post and holds it parallel to the ground,
     facing the aiming circle.

STEP 6

     C-8. The “circle” Soldier measures the mil angle between the two strips of tape on the
     post (subtense bar) and records the mil reading along with the azimuth to TS-1. (See
     figure C-8.)

STEP 7

     C-9. The post then is replaced into the ground and the “circle” Soldier moves forward
     to this point and sets up the aiming circle directly over this point. This completes the
     first traverse leg.

STEP 8

     C-10. This procedure is repeated until the desired mortar position is reached. The
     information obtained may either be written down as an azimuth, a mil angle, and a TS,
     or a diagram may be constructed. (See figure C-9.) To avoid confusing others working
     with a hasty survey, any diagram should reflect the route of the various traverse legs and
     should be close to scale.

482         TC 3-22.91 (INCL C1)  29 August 2025


---

                Field-Expedient Survey Techniques

                            Figure C-9. Construction of a diagram

STEP 9

     C-11. The information recorded by the “circle” Soldier goes to the FDC either as the
     traverse legs are made or after all the legs have been completed. The beginning known
     point is represented by the pivot point of the M16 plotting board.

STEP 10

     C-12. Starting at the pivot point, the data are applied on the board for each leg of the
     hasty survey. For example:

          • The azimuth on the first traverse leg was 5790 mils; index that information on
                the M16 plotting board.

          • The distance between the two strips of tape on the aiming posts was 18.5 mils.
          • See the distance table (see figure C-10, page 484) for the 2-meter subtense bar

                width:
                ▪ A mil angle of 18.5 mils is equal to a distance of 110 meters.
                ▪ For the hasty survey, make one square on the plotting board equal to

                     25 meters.
          • From the pivot point on the direction of 5790 mils, move 110 meters (4 2/5

                squares) along the index line, place a dot, and circle it; this point, marked as
                TS-1, completes traverse leg 1.
          • The azimuth for the second traverse leg was 4786 mils; index this information
                on the plotting board.
          • At TS-2, the mil angle measured for the 2-meter subtense bar width was 10.1
                mils.

29 August 2025  TC 3-22.91 (INCL C1)  483


---

Appendix C

          • See the distance table for the 2-meter subtense bar width; 10.1 mils equals a
                distance of 200 meters.

          Figure C-10. Distance table for a 2-meter subtense bar

     • With 4786 mils indexed on the plotting board, move up 200 meters from TS-1
          along or parallel to a vertical line (eight squares), place a dot, and circle it.

     • This point, marked TS-2, completes traverse leg 2, repeat the same procedure
          for traverse legs 3, 4, and 5.

     • Rotate the M16 plotting board until TS-5 (mortar position) is directly over the
          vertical centerline.

     • Read the azimuth from the top of the plotting board; this is the direction from
          the known starting point to the base mortar squad’s position.

     • Count the number of squares along the index line between the pivot point and
          TS-5:
          ▪ Each square equals 25 meters.
          ▪ This is the straight-line distance from the known starting point to the base
               mortar squad’s position.

     • If given data were properly applied in the example, a known starting point-base
          mortar squad azimuth should have been obtained of 5961 mils, and a known
          starting point-based mortar squad distance of 690 meters (+/-5 mils and 10
          meters).

     • Apply these data to the map:
          ▪ From the known starting point along the direction of 5961 mils, move 690
               meters.

                                                            Field-Expedient Survey Techniques

     ▪ The new point is the eight-digit grid coordinate for the base mortar squad’s
          position.

                                        Appendix D

            Fire Direction Center Certification

       FDC certification is required for all units with mortars; it tests the
       proficiency of Soldiers to perform their duties as FDC computers and
       section leaders. This appendix provides the commander with a means to
       verify that Indirect Fire Infantryman are trained in FDC procedures. All
       FDC personnel and those who perform FDC operations are subject to
       certification. A set of example tests are provided to assist in the development
       of unit certification programs. Units based their certification program on
       their METL and assigned equipment. A certified FDC, certified mortar
       crew, and a certified FO are required for live fire training.
       The mortar training strategy can be found in TC 3-22.90.

  CONDUCT OF THE PROGRAM

        D-1. The lethality of mortars on the battlefield and their ability to set conditions
        requires the highest standards of proficiency. To ensure accurate organic indirect fires,
        the unit’s FDC certification minimums should require 70 percent of all section leaders,
        squad leaders, and FDC personnel pass the FDC certification. Certification should be
        conducted every 6 months or during the training and sustaining cycles, of the appropriate
        force generation pool, following the unit’s execution plan.

        D-2. The FDC certification program should be conducted as centralized training at the
        regiment or brigade level. Commanders select an examination board made up of

+ experienced senior indirect fire Infantryman who are preferably Infantry Mortar Leader

        Course graduates. The examination board is responsible for the set-up, conduct and
        certification of the program. The FDC certification program includes a hands-on skills
        test and a system-based test conducting seven FDC missions.

                Note. Considerable training value can be obtained by using a centralized
                evaluation and by obtaining the experience of several units’
                noncommissioned officers.

        D-3. The examination board is responsible for the following:
             • Reviewing required Soldier certification tasks (tasks listed on page 487).
             • Developing mission set up information (missions listed on page 488).
             • Developing all scoresheets and answer keys.
             • Developing all secondary and retest material.
             • Safeguarding FDC certification program testing information.
             • Serving as the arbitration committee to hear and decide on testing grievances.

        D-4. The examination board refers to chapter 6 for plotting board, chapter 10 for
        MFCS, or chapter 7 for LHMBC.

29 August 2025  TC 3-22.91 (INCL C1)  487


---

  Appendix D

 PERSONNEL SUBJECT TO CERTIFICATION

       D-5. Soldiers in the following positions are subject to be evaluated for certification:
             • FDC computer.
             • Section leader.
             • Squad leaders who perform FDC operations (Cavalry, brigade combat team,
                  and so on).
             • FDC RTO.

               Note. All sergeants and the above should be administered an FDC
               certification, but only personnel that perform FDC operations certifies for
               live-fire operations. A squad leader’s failure to pass the FDC certification
               does not preclude the squad from firing if that squad operates under a certified
               controlling FDC.

 GENERAL RULES

       D-6. The FDC certification program is designed to be a program that the commanders
       can use to certify FDC personnel. Battalions provide scorers (staff sergeant and above)

+ who are preferably Infantry Mortar Leader Course graduates to grade units other than

       their own. The goal is to certify under a standardized evaluation program with qualified
       leaders, who are internal to the parent organization. Conditions should be the same for
       all Soldiers during the certification. The examining board ensures that information
       obtained by a Soldier during testing is not passed to another Soldier. The examining
       board ensures consistency of the certification across the entire regiment/brigade.
       Validation of unit mortar SOPs are secondary objective. However, the examining board
       adjudicates issues between testing requirements and SOPs.

 QUALIFICATION

       D-7. Soldiers pass the hands-on certification tasks for each system before conducting
       the FDC certification missions. To be certified, Soldiers score a minimum of 70 percent
       on each of the seven missions listed for each system assigned to the unit. The goal of
       hands-on testing is the demonstration of skills required to operate all FDC systems
       assigned to the unit. The goal of mission testing is demonstrating the skills required to
       perform duties as the FDC computer and providing the correct firing data to the mortar
       crews. The Soldier certifies using the primary FDC computer and the M16- and
       M19-plotting board. Soldiers may retest only once on any part of the test they have
       failed. Soldiers who fail the retest will not be certified and will be required to repeat the
       FDC certification program during the next evaluation.

 CERTIFICATION TASKS

       D-8. Certain criteria are used to test the Soldier’s ability to perform hands-on tasks
       required to operate each FDC system. The certification includes, but is not limited to,
       the tasks listed for each system.

488  TC 3-22.91 (INCL C1)  29 August 2025


---

                Fire Direction Center Certification

                                      Appendix E

            Mortar Qualification DA Forms

    This appendix describes the use of the mortar crew roll-up and mortar crew
    fire mission scoresheet DA forms. These forms were developed to bring
    transparency and accountability to mortar training and qualification. These
    forms are digitally fillable and digitally signable. They are the required
    records used to capture mortar training Tables I through VI and any mortar
    validation event.

MORTAR CREW ROLL-UP

     E-1. The mortar crew for qualification includes the key leaders, mortar fire direction
     personnel, and mortar system squads. Mortar formations qualify as their entire
     formation. A mortar platoon qualifies as a platoon, not as sections, or squads. A
     company or troop mortar section qualifies as a section, not as squads.

     E-2. There are two separate mortar crew roll-up DA forms. DA Form 7882 (Mortar
     Crew Platoon Roll-Up) is used to record the crew qualification of a mortar platoon.
     DA Form 7883 (Mortar Crew Section Roll-Up) is used to record the crew qualification
     of a company or troop mortar section. Each form has two pages, front and back. These
     forms are available on the Army Publishing Directorate website. See figures E-1 through
     E-4, pages 492 through 495.

29 August 2025  TC 3-22.91 (INCL C1)  491

     Figure E-1. DA Form 7882 (Mortar Crew Platoon Roll-Up) page 1

                                      Mortar Qualification DA Forms

Figure E-2. DA Form 7882 (Mortar Crew Platoon Roll-Up) page 2

     Figure E-3. DA Form 7883 (Mortar Crew Section Roll-Up) page 1

                                      Mortar Qualification DA Forms

    Figure E-4. DA Form 7883 (Mortar Crew Section Roll-Up) page 2

E-3. Different mortar formations have differing personnel across brigade combat team
types. These forms are used for all Army mortar formations. Mortar leaders will fill out
the administrative data for their unit and their mortar formation personnel.

E-4. When a duty position is not filled, the mortar leader will leave the RANK, LAST,
FIRST, MIDDLE INITIAL cell blank. When a duty position is not authorized by

29 August 2025  TC 3-22.91 (INCL C1)  495


---

Appendix E

     modified table of organization and equipment (MTOE) for that mortar formation, the
     mortar leader will write in the RANK, LAST, FIRST, MIDDLE INITIAL cell “NOT
     AUTHORIZED BY MTOE.”

     E-5. These DA forms are updated after the completion of each table. The mortar leader
     completes the TABLE I-A GUNNER’S EXAMINATION column by checking the
     block YES if the Soldier passed or NO if the Soldier did not pass, then input the date of
     the event. The mortar leader completes the TABLE I-B MORTAR FIRE DIRECTION
     EXAMINATION by checking the block YES if the Soldier passed or NO if the Soldier
     did not pass, then input the date of the event.

     E-6. The mortar leader completes the INFANTRY MORTAR LEADER COURSE
     column by filling in the date after viewing the Soldier’s certificate of graduation from
     the Infantry Mortar Leader Course. Leave the date blank if the Soldier has not graduated
     from the Infantry Mortar Leader Course.

     E-7. The mortar leader completes the TABLE II, SITUATIONAL TRAINING
     EXERCISE, VIRTUAL by checking the block YES if the Soldier was present at and
     participated in the training and NO if the Soldier was not present at or did not participate
     in the training. The mortar leader then fills in the training facility where the Table II
     training was conducted; Call for Fire Trainer, Virtual Battlespace, Combat Close
     Tactical Trainer, or Training Area.

MORTAR CREW FIRE MISSION SCORESHEET

     E-8. The mortar formations must be able quickly and accurately engage enemy targets
     using all authorized mortar analog and digital fire control systems. There is a training
     and evaluation outline for each type of mortar fire mission. The mortar formation is
     evaluated on their ability to accurately engage targets first in a mortar ammunition dry
     status during Table IV, then in a mortar ammunition live fire status in Table VI. The
     mortar crew fire mission scoresheet used to record the results of which fire missions
     where evaluated, who received the fire mission and computed mortar firing data, and on
     which mortar analog or digital fire control system was used to compute the fire mission.

     E-9. There are two separate mortar fire mission scoresheet DA forms. DA Form 7881
     (Mortar Crew Platoon Fire Mission Scoresheet) is used to record the fire missions of a
     mortar platoon during Tables IV and VI. DA Form 7880 (Mortar Crew Section Fire
     Mission Scorecard) is used to record the fire missions of a company or troop mortar
     section during Tables IV and VI. These forms are available on the Army Publishing
     Directorate website. See figures E-5 through E-8, pages 497 through 500.

496  TC 3-22.91 (INCL C1)  29 August 2025
